EA
In the course of a telephone conversation today,
Mr. Jansen, Chief of the Metals and Minerals Division
of the Bureau of Foreign and Domestio Commerce, mentioned
894.24/942
to me that during the last two months Japan had fallen
from first to third place as a buyer of United States
iron and steel scrap and that he believed the explanation
of this decline is that the Japanese are running short
894.24
of funds. As evidence, he cited the fact that Japanese
are now endeavoring to persuade American sorap dealers
to sell them sorap on thirty, sixty, and ninety day oredit.
He inquired what reply we were giving to business men who
asked us whether or not they should extend credit in
connection with exports to Japan. I replied that we were
informing suoh inquirers that, 1n view of Japan's finan-
cial position, 1t would appear to be wiser not to extend
credit to Japanese interests at this time.
Charlos W. Yost

Japan regarding which we had conference with Mr. Berle and you at
Washington recently and subsequent telephone talks with you, we are
quoting below the ceble sent to Mr. Saneyoshi:
"CHICAGO MAY 24 1940
SANEYOSHI
ANTIKNOCK
TOKYO (Japan)
894.
WITH ALL DUE REGARD AND HIGHEST RESPECT FOR YOU PERSONALLY
SITUATION HAS CHANGED SO SUBSTANTIALLY AND HAS BECOME SO
CRITICAL SINCE CABLING YOU APRIL 27TH AND 3OTH THAT WE NOW
ARE ASKED TO DEVOTE OUR ENTIRE TIME AND AVAILABLE FACILITIES
TO SUBSTANTIAL INCREASE CRACKING CAPACITY AND SUPPLY OF
AVIATION GASOLINE HERE WHICH NOW PREVENTS FOR TIME BEING
COMPLETION WORK FOR MITSUI AND PLANS FOR FUJINAGATA. WE
HOPE THIS DEPLORABLE WORLD SITUATION MAY SOON RETURN TO
NORMAL RELATIONSHIP.
HALLE"

=== Page 54 === (confidence 0.98)
TIHTE IFLD - IC ITHGTOH  C
May 13 1940
And while we're standing on the brink of & possible
war with Japan, doesn't it seem the least bit bizarre
that we should also be shipping Japan a lot of the essen-
tial materials Japan must haye to
Bring-on
keep up its war in China?
For years, now, we've been sending
The Embargo
Japan scrap iron with which to make
bullets to shoot into our friends the Chinese. We are
now sending Japan scrap iron which may before long
be coming right back to lodge in the bellies or brain-
pans of some of our own American boys.
It doesn't seem quite right, somehow.
It seems so far from right or sane to ús that we can
see no further arguments against the imposition of the
much discussed embargo on all American war supply
shipments to Japan.
We have been legally entitled to slap such an
embargo on Japan since last January 26, when we
abrogated our commercial treaty with Tokyo. We've
argued about it enough, while the case in its favor has
gone on getting stronger and stronger. Why not stow
the arguments and take the plunge?
Meanwhile, for our safety's sake in all the oceans,
Two Ships for One, above, under and on the ocean.
(Copyright, 1940, News Syndicate Co., Inc.)

sir:
I am writing you to request that you use your good
offices in conveying to our State Department the
opinion of myself, and millions of other citizens,
that the long range policy of the United States in
international affairs should be directed toward the
secession of continuing to furnish materials of war,
either finished or crude, to Japan, thus enabling her
to further arm as a threat to world peace. I am making
this appeal on a striotly national - and a non-partisan
basis.

New York, N. Y., April 18 ... Japan's large secret naval construction
program, revealed recently in statements from ashington, has been made possible
by plentiful supplies of American materials, especially iron and steel scrap,
according to a statement made today by Harry B. Price, executive secretary of
the American Jommittee for Non-Participation in Japanese Aggression.
Based on Departaent of Commerce figures, the statement points out that
894.24/95
from 1934 to 1939 inclusive American exoorts of scrap iron and scran steel to
Japan reached a volume of 8,52 ,81ll tons - a total export greater than that to
all other nations conbined, includin; Great Britain, France, Germany, Russia,
and Italy. The statement follows:
5
"American materials, especially scran iron and scrap steel, and American
machinery, have made possible Japan's huge secret naval constriction program, ex-
ceeding in some categories that of the 'rited stntes itself, according to facts
made known in ashigton recently.
"During the six vears, 1934 to 193, inclusive, Janan obtained from the
United States 8,52 ,8ll tens of sore iron un scran steel, or 5" of Americn's
total export t nll contries of 16.498,96 tons. Tms American exports to Japan
of these materials, ?n iame tal for noval cnstr ction, have exceeded those to
Great Britain, Ger any, Fra ce, tissla, Italy, and all other countries combined,
according to statistics published by the "nited States Department of Commerce.
"In 193), 57% of all Americen exports of scrap iron and steel went to
Japan.
"In l938, according to careful studies, Japan obtained aporoximately
90% of her imnorts of these metals from the "nited States. The pronertion for

1939 was probably higher, although statistios of Japan's imports from other countries
in that year are not yet available; with other souroes restrioted, due to the
European conflict, Japan imported from the United States in 1939 an all-time high
of 2,022,958 tons.
"During the previous six-vear period, 1928 to 1933 inclusive, Japan's
purchases of these metals averaged 216,075 tons per year. In 1934, Japan denounced
her naval treaty with the United States and Great Britain and in that year her sorap
iron and steel purohases from the United States more than doubled. From that year
onward, her purchases of these metals have exceeded a million tons annually, averag-
ing approximately 1,420,000 tons per year, an increase of 527 per cent over the
previous six-year period. During the first two months of 1940, exports of sorap
iron and steel to Japan totaled 210,337 tons, aporoximately half of America's
exports to all countries of 42l,173 tons.
"Only a portion of these extensive exports of iron and steel scrap would
be needed for the construction of between 40), 000 and 500,000 tons of warships in
Japan's battle fleet now reported to be completed or nearing completion. The
balance has been available for the development of war industries and for the con-
duot of Jaoan's war agninst China.
"Thus the United States has f rnished indispensable help, not only for
the invasion of ihine, but also for the b:ilding up of a navy reported to be almost
equaling our ow: in strength. While this exoort trade continues, further depleting
valuable and strategic American resources, it becomes necessary to impose huge
additional tax burdens upon the nation or naval construction in order to meet the
competition which American exports nre making possihle.
"Pending legislation, introduced by Senators Pittman and Schwellenbach,
would authorize the placing of restrictions upen these strategic exports, on the
basis of Amerioan rights and obligations under the Nine Power Treaty."

=== Page 78 === (confidence 1.00)
THE AMERICAN COMMITTEE
FOR NON-PARTICIPATION IN JAPANESE AGGRESSION
8 West 4Oth Street, New York, N. Y.
Washington Office
1420. New York Avenue, N. W., Washington, D. C.
May 17, 1940.
Dear Mr. President:
I offer my heartiest congratulations on your in-
spiring address to the Congress yesterday. Apart from
the wisdom and necessity of the measures which you
proposed, the vigorous leadership which you have given
the country in this emergency will have a moral effect
of profound significance.
As you may remember, our committee, of which
Colonel Henry L. Stimson is honorary chairman, was
formed to try to check the supply of war materials
from this country to Japan. We have supported the
general idea of a limited embargo without committing
ourselves to any one of the seven bills introduced
into the Senate and House, one of them by a Republican
representative.
Now that the problem has become so much larger,
with the extension of the war in Europe, it has seemed
to us that it might be wiser to work for a law that
would enable the President to withhold from export any
basic war materials and equipment as a means of con-
serving our national resources and ensuring that what
we produce is used for the welfare of our own country
and the protection of its interests, instead of being
used sometimes against us as at present. Such a measure
should of course contain provision for permitting the
export of the goods in question when the Government is
satisfied that they would be used for purposes compatible
with American national interests.
The
The President
The White House
Washington, D. C.

JOHN NORRIS MYERS

Ridgewood, N. J.
June 1, 1940
The Honorable Parnell Thomas
United States House of Representatives
Washington, D. C.
Dear Mr. Thomas:
Why on earth, with the example of the mistakes
1n Europe, can't we stop making the same mistakes in the
Far East? Why don't we stop feeding scrap iron, airplane
parts and other munitions to Japan, which is using them
to slaughter Chinese women and children? There 1s ample
authority to do this under our present laws and the women
of our club are confident that even a word request to
manufacturers by the State Department would bring prompt
compliance.
Please read the article in the Reader's Digest for
May, 1940 by Nathanial Peffer, ent1tled "Our Job 1n the
Far East." This describes better than I can the feelings
of most thinking people in America.
For God's sake, stop this trade before China 1s
stamped out and divert these supolies to help France and
England stop Hitler.
This is the hope not only of my own woman's club,
but I believe of the women of America.
Sincerely yours,
RUTH A. MYERS
(Mrs. John Norris Myers)

June 8, 1940
The ionorable Franklin D. Roosevelt
President of the United States
Washington, D. C.
Dear Mr. Fresident:
There is an opportunity in montana to make a considerable amount
of money by shi pin: scrap iron to Japan at this time for the rea-
son that it appears that this commodity is in great demand, but
under ordinary conditions we are so isolated by freight rates it
has never had a chance to be moved with profit. Today I would
estimate there is some fifty to one hundred thousand tons of scrap
laying in Montana, yoming and Idaho. while I could personally
profit from the sale of this commodity, i do not think it patriotic
to do so and I am unwilling to participate.
we also hear it rumored that there is considerable talk in washing-
ton about placing an embargo on this scrap iron and steel to japan,
whicn is no doubt being done by the major steel producers. I do
feel that if an embargo is placed on scrap iron that it should
also be placed on steel, copper, oil, cotton and every material
that can be used ror war purposes by an aggressor nation and 1
do think we slould have placed such an embargo against Japan
several years ago and now there are several other countries which
fit into the same class, inciuding Italy.
very truly yours,
. b. Coolidge.

BHOULD THE UNITED STATEB "APPEASE" JAPAN?
With the steadily inoreasing gravity of the situation in Europe, the demend
has been made that we patoh up our differenoes with Japan in the hope that this
would free us from any possible involvement in the Paoifio as the outlock in the
Atlantio beoomes more and more serious. The argument is that if we make friends
with Japan we can eafeguard our "baok door" while preparing for possible eventual-
ities elsewhere. If the policy advocated had a real chanoe of suocess, one might
maintain that it was warranted in view of the extreme gravity of the situation
in Burope. But thoughtful analysis shows, we believe, that there are dangerous
fallacies in suoh a polioy of attempted appeasement now.

the United Stutes

by betraying China, or abandoning our own prinoiples.
(b) It would be a complete reversal of our historio polioies in the Paoifio
and would be interpreted by the Japanese as e sign of fear. Just as the
egreement at Munioh, at the expense of Czeoho-Slovakia, made Hitler more
bold and inoreased his prestige at home, just so would Amerioan oonoessions
to Japan, at the expense of Chine, increase the confidence and arroganoe
of Japan's military leaders, and strengthen their hold upon the Japanese
people. Fer from inoreasing our own aegurity in the Paoifio. suoh sotion

(e) Reoent experienoe furnishes no assuranoe that an, pledges made by Japan's

(f) If, on the other hand, we ohoose this moment when Japan is relatively

Mr. Wilbur, Washington representative of the Aluminum
Corporation of America, telephoned me today and inquired
894.24/963
whether the "moral embargo" applied to the export of aluminum
oxide to Japan. I told Mr. Wilbur that I believed that
aluminum oxide was subject to the policy in question but,
in order to make certain, I would consult my superiors and
call him back. Mr. Wilbur informed me that aluminum oxide
was the "secondary form" in the making of aluminum metal
and that by a certain treatment it could be turned into
aluminum metal.
I consulted Mr. Yost on this matter. He said that
he felt that this material was subject to the moral embargo
and that it would be advisable to call Mr. Wilbur's atten-
FILED
tion to the legislation now pending in Congress whioh might
1940
subject the export of this material to certain legal re-
strictions. I called Mr. Wilbur back and informed him ao-
cordingly.

warring countries including Japan.
Our organization includes 178 out of an or1-
ginal company of 265-95% of whom have been wounded in combat
at least onoe, and many two and three times, know the hor-
rors of war, and wish to call the attention of the nation
to the dangers of these large shioments of thousands and
thousands of tons of scran-metal leaving our shores.
A movement such as ors needs the co-operation
and support of every American who have the interest of the
welfare and security of our united nation.
With both feet on the ground, well balanoed,
and with no desire to become alarmistsin any sense of the
word, we feel and belleve, we should not be caught napping,
with our trousers down.
Ever ready to be of service to our nation,
our comrades in arms, thoughtful of our loved ones, and
realizing that we are too old to bear arms in a foreign
country, but willing to defend our homes at all costs,
and despite the disoredit of the Super-Ultra Patriotic
National Economy League, let us awake to the danger of a
grouo of war mongers who see nothing but profit and power
at the exoense of the oream of the American possessions,
"OUR YOUTH".
Your brother Edd1e who served with us in "B"
Co. exemolifies the spirit and character of all who served

time.

from the Embassy at Tokyo.
MEMORANDUM
With reference to the Department's strictly confidential
instruction no. 1940 of April 18, 1940, the following informa-
tion is available regarding Bolivian ore shipments to Japan
and smelting facilities in Japan and "Manchukuo":
The American Consul at Kobe has reported that the Mitsu-
bishi interests own mines at Akenobe and Ikuno which produce
about two thousand metric tons of tin ore annually. The
same company has a smelter at Ikuno with a capacity of four
hundred tons of ore caily (This is probably an error for
four hundred tons of ore monthly).
The Consul at Kobe reported further that five hundred
tons of Bolivian tin arrived at Kobe on April 16 from Anto-
fagasta, which was unuerstood to be destined for Vladivostok.
In addition there have been shipments of copper and antimony
ore fro1 Chile, lead from Manzanillo, and forty-two tons of
molybdenite from Callao, all of which is likewise reported to
be destined for Vladivo:.ok.
The Consul at Osaka has reported that refined tin is
produced in that district, but that present production figures
are not available. In 1936, however, the production in Osaka
prefecture amounted to about fifteen hundred metric tons.
The Osaka Consulate reports further that there have been
reports from a reliable source of transshipments at Osaka
of American copper and lead, which were landed at Osaka
on open consignment and then transferred to Japanese traap
steamers bound for Vladivostok. A press ban has been issued
recently forbidding the publication of news concerning any
suoh transactions relating to goods destined for Germany.
With

=== Page 130 === (confidence 0.99)
-2
With reference to the five hundred tons of Bolivian
ore referred to by the Consul at Kobe, the Assistant Com-
mercial Attaché at Tokyo states that this shipment was report-
ed to be destined to a firm in isinking named "Nisshisan"
or "Nisshinsan", but that no such firm can be located. The
British Embassy has made a formal request to the Foreign
Office for assurances that the shipment was actually intended
for a Manchurian firm for snelting and not intended for ship-
ment to Germany by way of Siberia.
The Assistant Commercial Attaché reports further that
there are three tin smelters in Japan, one at Ikuno near
Kobe, one at Uita in Kyushu, and one at Ashio. The total
combined capacity of the three smelters is estimated at 350
metric tons a month, or 4,200 metric tons annually. It is
further understood that the Bolivian Banco Minero is under
contract to supply all of the ore it collects to a British
firm in London, but as the latter is interested only in ore
having a definite percentae of tin, the British firm has
raised no objection to the sule of the lower grade ores to
Japanese interests. According to reports received from
British sources in Bolivia, Japanese interests are now en-
deavoring to conclude contracts wit. the Banco Minero for its
entire supply of low grade ores.
Conclusion
It would thus appear that the total annual capacity of
Japanese tin smelters is between four and five thousand metric
tons a year, that the total production of tin ore in Japan
is about two thousand metric tons annually, that low grade
tin

=== Page 131 === (confidence 0.99)
-3-
tin ore is being bought from Bolivia, and that trans-
shipments are being made of various metals and ores
from the Americas to Germany by way of Japan and
Vladivostok.
W. Garland Richardson
American Vice Consul.
June 6, 1940.

MEMORANDUM ON JAPAN'S DEPENDENCE ON AMERICAN SCRAP
Prepared by Chinese Counoil For Economic Research
U. S. Exports to Japan of Sorap Iron and Steel
American merchants sold to Japan in 1939 over 2 million tons (2,026,764) of
sorap iron and steel valued at almost 33 million dollars ($32,592,500)1
sorap exports to Japan were larger in 1939 than in any previous year, due undoubt-
edly to rush orders by Japan in anticipation of a possible ombargo following the
termination of tho Japanese-Amorican trado treaty in January, 1940.
In 1937, the first year of the Sino-Japanese War, the shipments of iron and
steel scrap to Japan greatly advanced over the average for the three previous
years. The following index numbers (Average 1934-36 = 100) graphically reveal
the trend of U. S. shipments for the three years prior to the War and for the
subsequent yoars.
Average
1934-36
1937
1938
1939
Quantity
100
171
124
182
Value
100
300
168
249
The trend of U. S. shipments of scrap iron and steel to Japan in 1940 has
been downward. For the first 3 months of 1940 shipments were less by 50 percent
than they were for the same period in 1939. The scrap exports to Japan from Jan-
uary through March amounted to 526,000 tons in 1939 and to 252, 000 tons in 1940.
The urgent need of Japan to conserve its foreign exchange probab ly accounts for
the reduced purchases of serap by Japan during the early months of 1040.
During the 15 years prior to 1923, Canada was the largest market for Ameri-
can exports of scrap iron and steel. 2
In the succeeding ten years Japan has
1
Statistics on United States exports to Japan furnished by the Department of
Commerce.
2
Hearings before Senate Sub-committeo on Mili:ary Affairs on S.2025 and S. J.
Res 180 April 5, 1938, part 2, p. 19.

=== Page 134 === (confidence 0.99)
2
advanced to the position of the leading market for American sorap. In l939 more
than half (57 percent) of the U. S. shipments of sorap iron and steel went to
Japan.
The proportion of scrap exports to the total U. S. exports to Japan has been
inereasing in recent years. The value of the American scrap exports to Japan ad-
vanced from 6.3 percent of the total U. S. exports to Japan during the years
1934-1936 to 13.7 percent in 1937, 9.2 percent in 1938 and 14.1 percent in 1939.
Scrap is important in the manufacture of iron and steel in the United States.
The quantities of scrap in relation to the ore used in the iron and steel industry
in this country demonstrates the importance of this product in the American economy.
According to a report issued by the Bureau of Mines,
"The total sorap consumed in 1938 was equivalent to 132 percent
of the iron content of all domestic and foreign iron and manganifer-
ous iron ores used in blast furnaces, and purchased scrap alone equaled
62 percent of the iron content of the ores; in l937 the comparable per-
centages were 119 and 57."
The report further points out the increased use of scrap in American industry
when it states that:
"In making the average ton of steel in 1938 more scrap and less
pig iron were used than in 1937; a decline in the relative use of pur-
chased sorap was more than offset by an increase in the relative use
of home scrap. In iron furnaces the relative use of purchased scrap
increased, that of home scrap changed little, and that of pig iron de-
creased."1
The Bureau of Mines made a survey in the fall of 1939 of the supply within
the United States of iron and steel scrap and reported 7,220,O00 gross tons on
hand as of September 30, 1939. The Bureau further reported scrap consumption
for the month amounting to 3,078,100 gross tons "indicating known stocks held by
suppliers and consumers were equivalent to a ten week's supply."
It appears that under ordinary peace time circumstances, the United States
is not confronted with any major problem of adequate supply. However, large
scale exports of iron and steel scrap would be seriously reflected in "a national
1
Statistical and Economic Survey, M M S No. 770, dated Dec. 4, 1939, Bureau of
Mines.

=== Page 135 === (confidence 0.99)
3
mergeney requiring peak production". Iron foundries conveivably would be "handi-
capped by the lack of essential scrap." In the last war and during subsequent
oritical periods, the country was confronted with a shortage of scrap.1
Japan's Import of Scrap Iron and Steel
Japan depended upon non-yen foreign countries for over 50% of its total
sorap consumption. Two and a quarter million tons of iron and steel scrap valued
at 36 million dollars were imported into Japan in 1939from 14 countries.2
Almost nine-tenths (89 percent) of the tonmage (as distinct from value) came
from the United States. A dozen other countries furnished tho remaining 10
percent.
Since the beginning of the Far Eastern Var, the United States' share of the
quantity of scrap iron and steel exports to Japan increased from 84 percent in
1937, to 86 percent in 1938 and to 89 percent in 1939. The following table pre-
sents the tonnage (metric tons)3 of scrap iron and steel shipment to Japan of all
countries and the share of the United States in the total:

upon the United States for her foreign supply of iron and steel scrap becomes

Borap in the Japanese Iron and Steel Industry
Scrap is derived from outworn iron and steel produots and from wastes
which accumlate in the course of production. Heavy consumption of scrap is
charaoteristic of iron and steel manufacture in Japan. This is explained by
the preponderance of open-hearth furnaces for which scrap is used in high
percentages because of economic reasons. The use of scrap in Japan began to
inorease in 1932 until an impressive total of 3,337,000 metric tons was re-
corded for the year 1936. This trend is considerably accelorated under the
stimulus of the prosent expansionist policy followed by the heavy industrics.1
Concerning the importan@ of scrap for tho Japanoso steol industry, the United
States Bureau of Minos in the Mincral Yoarbook for 1939 states that,
"The production of steel ingots and casting in Japan
has moro than doubled since 1932, and the domand for ferrous
raw materials has increased correspondingly. Pig-iron pro-
duction has been oxpandod to mect part of the requiromonts;
howovor, the proportions of scrap used in furnacc chargos
also have incroased, and imports of scrap iron and scrap
stool aro ssential to the maintenance of the presest steol-
making rate."
The amount of scrap Japan uses in relation to pif iron in the course of
producing iron and steel products was indicated in the U. S. Commerce Dopart-
ment's Japan Monthly Trade Report of November 25, 1939, whore it was stated
that in the face of an "anticipated reductior in scrap iron imports following
the expiration of the commercial treaty with the Unitod States in January, 1940,
the Japan Steel Materials Federation is reported to have drawn up a plan for the
production of steel without the need of large quantities of imported scrap. The
scheme calls for the use of "larger quantities of pig iron." However, "in the
meantime," the Report continued, "the Federation established a maximum allot-
ment of 60% scrap and 40% pig for thc fourth quarter ( of 1939 ) in order to
conserve existing stocks of raw materials. Trade reports state that the allot-
ments will be further reduced to a maximum of 50% scrap iron from the beginning
1/ See Japan-Manchoukuo Year Book, 1940, p. 340.

=== Page 140 === (confidence 0.99)
6
1940, and in the event that there are any developments which would lead to
a cessation of imports from the United States, it is claimed that it would be
necessary for domestic mills to opcrate on a basis of 20% scrap and 80% pig."
It is very likely that Japanose steel mills are at prosont operating on tho
basis of 50% sorap and 50% pig. Any further drastic scaling down in the scrap
peroentago will entail great difficulties in connection with readjustments, in
view of tho fact that tho majority of steel manufacturing companies rely on
open-hearth furnaces, which must use scrap.1
Alternatives to American Scrap
If American iron and steel scrap should become unavailable to Japan she
would be confronted with three alternatives: (A) Replace American scrap with
other foreign scrap; (B) Curtail production; (c) Obtain pig iron and iron ore.
These alternatives are considered in the following discussion.
Alternative (A): Replacement of American Scrap with other Foreign
Scrap - Foreign scrap constitutes more than half of the scrap consumed by Japan.
Ninety percent of the crap imported into Japan comes from the United States.
The stocks of the countries furnishin Japan with scrap, with the exception of
the United States, are very limited. Moreover, since the European War, restric-
tions and prohibitions have been placeu upon the exports of scrap by most of the
countries making shipmonts to Japan. It is an unavoidablc conclusion that Japan
today depends more than ever upon the United States for iron and stoel scrap and
that it would be impossible for her to replace the 2,O00,Ooo tons reccived from
the United States in other markets.
Alternative (B): Curtailment of Production - E. . Pehrson of the
United States Bureau of Mines ostimates that one-third of Japan's steel pro-
duction in 1937 dopended upon American scrap.2 It may be safely assumed that

=== Page 141 === (confidence 0.99)
7
this was also true of 1938 and 1939. The loss of steel output in consequence
of an American embargo on exports of scrap to Japan would play havoc with the
latter's war program. Japan's decision in the event of a cessation of sorap
supplies from the United States would probably be to make a despearte effort to
locate alternative supplies of pig iron and iron ore in order to avoid stoppages
of production in tho war and allied industries which would greatly affoct her
campaigns in China as well as her other expansionist designs.
Alternativo (C): Obtaining Pig Iron and Iron Ore to Replace American scrap
1. Procurement of Pig Iron - The Japanese Empire doubled its pig
iron production botwoon 1931 and 1936, the output for the latter yoar being
over 2 million tons which was insufficient to keep pace with the "demands
of the steel works".1 In succeoding years production increases continued
attaining a lovol of 3.3 million tons in 1939.2
In 1936, the last year for which official figures are available, 63.3
percent of the pig iron available for consumption was produced in Japan Proper.
The colonies and Manchuria between them contributed 15 per cent. The Empire
as a whole (including Manchuria) furnishod 78.3 per cent of the available
pig iron, with the remaining 21.7 per cent obtained by importation. The
following data, in thousands of metric tons, presents the pig iron production

We will now review briefly the present status of the various supply
souroes, including both the yen and the non-yen areas.
Manohuria - Manchuria has been an important source of pig iron, contribut-
ing, as the above table shows, over 8 percent of the amount available for
consumption in Japan in 1936. The production of pig iron in Manchuria has
inoreased during the past few years, but the amounts shipped to Japan have de-
clined.1 The increased Manchurian output has tended to be absorbed by local
steel works.
Chosen - Pig iron production in Chosen has averaged approximately
200,000 tons a year from 1934-1939. It is questionable if Chosen will be in
a position to make marked increases of pig iron available to Japan in 1940.
China - The output of pig iron in China Proper is known to be small and
no exportable surplus can be expected.
British India - British India during the Sino-Japanese War has been the
leading foreign source of pig iron for Japan. More than half of the country's
production has been going to Japan, the remainder to Greut Britain. Ship-
ments declined in 1937 over 1936, increased in 1938 and declined somewhat in
1939. Since September 25, 1939 the government of India has required license
for all future exports of pig iron. This is most likely to mean a reduction
of shipments to Japan. In January 1940 Indian shipments delivered to Japan
were 15 percent less than shipments for January 1939.

Soviet Union - In 1935 and 1936, the Soviet Union made fairly large
shipments of pig iron to Japan, but since then, because of the strained
relations between the two countries due to Japan's signing the Anti-
Commintern Pact towards the close of 1936, there has been no record of any
shipment nor is the Soviet source promising in the immediato future.
U. S. A. - Until 1937 the U. S. was not an important source of pig iron
for Japan. But in that year shipments suddenly reached 409 thousand tons valued
at almost 10 million dollars. The Tariff Commission in its report points out that
"the explanation of this abrupt shift in l937 lies in a combination of factors,
but chiefly the utilization of Manchurian stocks locally, curtailment in the sup-
ply from the Soviet Union, and competition of British buyers for the Indian output.
1
American shipments in 1938 declined to 316 thousand tons and in 1939
dropped to less than 1O thousand tons. Since the U. S. is more carefully
scrutinizing its strategic materials and machine tools shipments have
already been barred to Japan, it is not likely that the U. S. will again
become a major source of pig iron for Japan.
Australia - While Australia has been producing fairly large amounts of
pig iron, only small quantities have been exported. In l939, for the first
time, Australia exported to Japan 32,400 tons of pig iron, almost one-half of
the country's entire export of this product. As regards the immediate future, in
view of her ovm pressing wartime demands for ferrous materials, Australia can
hardly be counted as a continuing source of pi iron for Japan.
European Countries - European courtries have been a minor source of pig iron
for Japan in the past, but in 1939 there was no official record of any shipments
by any of these countries. Under the present war conditions this source must be
regarded as closed to Japan.

=== Page 144 === (confidence 0.99)
10
Thus the oountries outside the yen bloc which in the past have furnished
Japan with pig iron are presently involved in war or so out of sympathy
with Japan's campaign in China that these sources of supply will be less
available or completely closed to Japan at least in the foreseeable future.
The prospects of increased shipments from Manchuria and Chosen also appear
none too bright. Under these circumstances Japan must resort to making pig
iron herself, and the problem will be boiled down to one of securing the
requisite amount of iron ore both from domestic and foreign sources.
2. Procurement of Iron Ore - The Japanese Empire is poorly endowed with
iron ore resources, with total actual reserves in Japan and Chose, exclusive of
very low-grade ore and iron sand, estimated to be about 8o million tons (approx-
imately half of this amount being in Japan Proper). It depends very heavily upon
foreign supplies of iron ore for its output of iron and steel products. The
Tariff Commission in its report on iron and steel points out that in "19l3 the

The Empire's scanty iron ore reserves and those in Manchuria are of
low grade with an average iron content of 35 to 45 percent. There are
authorities in the field who are of the opinion that it takes 3 to 4 tons
of Japan's domestically available iron ore to make one ton of pif iron. It
therefore would be a conservative estimate that if the 2 million tons of
American scrap were not available to Japan it would require 5 to 6 million tons
of domestic ore to replae it in the form of pig iron. With the present annual
output of iron ore in Japan Proper plus the iron ore available from Chosen

Now consider the foreign sources. There are four principal regins where
Japan must look for the required ore; these are British Malaya, Australia, the
Philippines and China.
British Lialaya is the most important source of iron ore for Japan. In
1937, it contributed 56 percent of the total exports of iron ore by all the
principal countries to Japan, and in l939 its shipments to the Island Empire
reached a high peak of 1,975,000 tons. lialayan ores are high grade containing
about 60 percent iron. With the Japanese interest owning and controlling the
larger mines, prectically all of the output in the country goes to Japan.
However, since the outbreak of the European War, metal exports have been pro-
hibited except under license. It is to be doubted that Japan could count upon
British Malaya for increased iron ore supply in the event of an American scrap
embargo.
Australia produces large amounts of iron ore yearly, of which o ly a small
portion is exported to Japan. However, Australia has been one of the four main

Summary
The salient facts brought forth by the for going discussion may be
summarized as follows:
1. American scrap is of vital importance to the Japanese heavy industry
and to the war economy as a whole. The scrap material furnished Japan by the
United States is responsible for approximately ono-third of Japan's steel
production.
2. If Japan should be denied access to the American sorap market, which
provides 90 percent of her imported iron and steel scrap, the required material

=== Page 148 === (confidence 0.99)
14
could not possibly be replaced in other markets. Nor could Japan acquire foreign
pig iron to replace the interrupted U. S. scrap supply.
3. The only alternative would be for Japan to secure the necessary iron
ore for processing pig iron herself. In so far as the foreign sources are
concerned, no prospeots are in sight, at least in the near future, of any
considerably increased supply available to Japan. Under the circumstances
Japan would be compelled to exploit, at great cost, her own low-grade deposits.
To cover the loss of some 2 million tons of American scrap would require 5 to
6 million tons of domestically mined ore. This means that Japan would have to
increase at least four-fold her current iron ore production, estimated at around
1.250,O00 tons. Actually, however, it is even doubtful whether Japan could
in the near future aohievo a gain of as much as double the existing output.
4. The United States urider ordinary peace time circumstances is not faced
with any major problem of adequate scrap supply. However, when peak production
is required in a national omergency, as is the case with the present execution
of the national defense program, the country will be confronted with a shortage
of scrap.

=== Page 169 === (confidence 0.99)
THE FAR EASTFRN SITUATION
Desirability of:  (1) Control of Exports of all War Materials and
Equipment; and (2) of Imposition of Additional Duties on Imports
from Japan.
1. The President has discretion under the act approved July
2, 1940, to control exports of war materials in the interest of
national security.
Reduction of supplies to actual or potential enemies is
as important for security as are adequate supplies for our own use.
Since Japan, Germany, and Italy are potential opponents,
we should gain by checking our exports of war materials or equip-
ment to those countries. This might cause actual deficiency in
their supplies or by raising prices elsewhere reduce their s tock
of foreign exchange.
The fact that embargoes alone may not be decisive is not
an adequate argument against them, since added to other factors
their effects may be important. Japanese dread of an embargo
suggests that it would have some value to us.
Would leniency or yielding to Japan produce good results?
Japan would probably give assurances of respect for cer-
tain interests of ours in the Far Fast. Pust experience suggests
that the Japanese army and navy would disregard such obligations
at will.
The Pritish have made important concessions to Japan in
North China without gaining any improvement in relations. More
severe pressure on Britain has followed those concessions.
If further concessions proved inadequate to cause a Chinese
collapse the Japanese would continue to be dissatisfied with British
and American policy.
If Chinese resistance ended, Jupan would be able to pro-
ceed more vigorously against other British and American interests in
East Asia, and would probably do so with little delay.
The above argument seems to indicate the wisdom of refusing
to yield to Japan, and the desirability of withholding further
economic aid by checking exports of war materials and by reducing
Japan's stock of foreign exchange through limitation of imports from
Japan. Continuance of such aid would increase the danger against
which we have to arm.
2. It is desirablc to avoid, if possible, violent reaction in
Japan. A gradual program, to be modified according to circumstances,
might prevent that result. The following steps are tentatively
suggested:

=== Page 170 === (confidence 0.98)
-2-
a. Inclusion of iron, steel, copper and scrap metal,
petroleum products, and motor vehicles and parts in the list
of articles for which export licenses are required, but contin-
uance for a time of the issuance of licenses for such exports,
except scrap iron and steel, the price of which has been arti-
ficially enhanced by foreign demands for war purposes. Scrap
iron lends itself well to immediate action since an embargo on
such exports is desired by independent steel producers as w ell
as by the majority of disinterested American citizens.
b. If Japan continues unfriendly or takes some new hostile
action, licenses for export for other articles may first be
delayed and later if necessary refused, one by one or several
at a time as circumstances may make desirable.
c. New or increased duties might be imposed on Japanese
products, especially luxury goods such as silk. Japan has
gone much further by controlling all imports and suspending
or reducing purchase of many of our products.
3. The power nd responsibility for action now rests with the
Administration. Fither ction or inaction involves risks.
4. Recent experience seems to justify the common belief that
in Europe inaction and attempted appeasement of aggressors proved
the more dangerous course. There is little e vidence if any that a
passive policy in the Far Fast would bring better results.
5. It is doubtful whether Japan can sustain a much more ex-
tensive campaign than at present vith larger consum:tion of materials
bought from abroad than the present operations in China require,
especially since constint anxiety about ossible confliet with
hussia compels tne maintenance of large reserves for that con-
tingeney. The Japanese combine great caution before action with
courage in a ctual combat.
6. The great positive advantage to the democratic povers of
a rejuvenated China attached to tneir side, as against a China
dominated by Japan or hussia should not be overlooked.
7. A decisive Japanese victory over China would cause Russia
to be more concerned about her Far Fastern defense and would there-
fore make her weaker in Europe and more inclined to cooperate there
with Germany.
8. The proposed suspension of traffic tirough Burma indicates
an urgent need for strengthening British morale by some independent
action of our own.
9. For the above reasons it is submitted that the time has
come for some such positive action as is here suggested, to be
supplemented as soon as possible by liberal financial aid to China.
toger . Eeeenm
Washington, July 18,1940.

Brockton, Massachusetts
July 13, 1940
Hon. Richard Wigglesworth
Dear Sir:
Isn't it possible for our statesmen to prevent
hard-hearted, unscrupulous Americans from sending scrap-iron -
thousands of tons a year - to the Japanese?
I thought Americans were not to sell war materials
to belligerents!
It seems such a dreadful thing for us, who have
been friends of the Chinese and of their efforts to make a
Republic - to allow our greedy business men to send material to
kill them.
Our missionaries and the Christian educated
Chinese feel so hurt to be used this way; for they believe they
could "win out" if we did not help the Japanese.
In this country many of our best people have
been sending money to China for years, to establish schools,
hospitals and churches to show them the way Christ wants men
to live, and now, the wicked Americans are helping to undo the
work for which our people have given sacrificially to build up.
So I am writing to ask if you will make an
earnest effort to stop this sending of war material to Japan?
Yours cordially,
(Mrs.) Florence W. Gurney

Of timely interest is Tokyo's despatch
no. 4788 of June 20, 1940, enclosing a trans-
lation of an article in a leading Japanese
eoonomic journal on dependence of Japan upon
1mports of scrap iron from the United States.
The article points out various factors
in the present situation among which are:
(1) It is anticipated that Japan's steel
business will be adversely affected by
diversion of Indian pig iron and Malayan
iron ore to Great Britain as a consequence
of the cutting off of ore shipments to Great
Britain from Scandinavia.
(2) Of Japan's annual scrap iron con-
sumption imports constitute thirty-five per-
cent. Eighty percent of the total comes from
the United States. Scrap iron consumption in
Japan equals approximately seventy percent of
Japan's total steel production. The stoppage
of scrap iron imports from the United States
would cut down the local steel production at
least fifteen percent.
(3) It is stated that not until the
end

=== Page 190 === (confidence 0.98)
Enclosure No. /to despatch
No.4788dated June20 1940,
from the Embassy at Tokyo.
Excerpt in translation of an article from the May 1 issue
of Diamond, Tokyo.
At the time when the European war broke out seven months
ago, apprehension was entertained more or less about the
importation of scrap iron from the United States, of pig
iron from British India and iron ore from the South Sees.
Fortunately, Indian pig and Meleyen ore have to date been
imported more smoothly than was feared at first. But now
that the Scandinavian countries, vhich have been supplying
a lerge emount of iron ore to Britain, are involved in the
European hostilities, british steel interests will be obliged
to buy pig iron from India more aggressively than previously.
This is sure to heve bearin's upon our steel business.
In our opinion, however, whether we cen buy scrap iron
from the United States without uny difficulty requires far
more careful consideration. Scr:r iron consumption here
in 1936 was 3,210,000 metric tons s ae inst steel output
of 5,320,000 metric tons. It means that the former account-
ed for as much es 62 per cent of the latter. It is sssumed
that the ratio must have been boosted to somethins like 70
per cent by now. of the agrrete screp iron consumption,
waste availeble in local foundrics then colver corr's
35 per cent, serap collueted in the hom  rket 30 per
cent, and imports 35 per cent. ind the United States was,
and still is, the herviect supplier of this key material,
shipping not less then 80 per cent of the total arriv le,
while other sources include British Indie, the Netherlands
East Indies, China, Menchoukuo end Australia. Experts
estimate

=== Page 191 === (confidence 0.98)
-2-
estimate that the stoppage of scrap iron imports from the
United States would cut down the local steel production at
least by 15 per cent. What reguires special attention in
this connection is not only our extreme dependence on the
Americen market but also the fact that no large amount of
scrap iron imports can be expected from eny other country.
9. In view of the rising wave of anti-Japanese agitation
in America, there is no gainsaying the eventuelity of a scrap
iron embargo as advocated by some diehards there. From
the purely economic point of view as well, prospects are
rather gloomy. First, home demand will increase conspicu-
ously elong with the prosperity of the steel industry in
that country stimulated by the aggrevation of the war across
the Atlantic. Seccnd, the Allies will rush to the American
scrap market in order to make ood for what they have lost,
or will lose, from the cessetion of iron ore shipments from
North Europe.
The result will be the further rise of the
scrap iron quotetions in the United States.
Thus considered,
even if the Washington Administration should refrain from
resorting to such a reckless ec ion as the proposed scrap
iron embargo, further shrinkege of our screp iron purcheses
across the Pecific would be inevitable.
Hence comes the
need of taking effective and uppropriete counter-measures.
Whet is fundementally importent is the elimination of
our dependence on the forei&n supply of scrap iron and the
active construction of ore refinin& eguipment. Not until
the end of 194l, however, cen we dispense to an appreciable
extent with the importation of serap iron. In order to
cope with the pressins situation, expedient measures will
have to be taken, including stricter economy in steel con-
sumption,

=== Page 192 === (confidence 0.97)
-3-
consumption, the more effective operation of the existing
blast furnaces and diplomatic efforts to secure scrap iron
purchases.

SIR:
I have the honor to refer to my confidential
Despatch No. 190 of July 15, 1940 (File 866.12/800),
entitled "Report of Projected Exportation of Scrap
Iron" and to inform the Department now that the
9
S. S."BoR" of Yugoslav registry arrived at Puerto
Colombia yesterday forenoon to take on a cargo of
scrap iron.
According to information obtained from Eduardo
L. Gerlein & Cia., the Barranquilla firm which is
acting as the ship's agent, the BOR has a net tonnage
FULED
of 2212 tons, carries a crew of 10 officers and 22
men, and its master is R. Popovic. The vessel is
bound for Osaka, Japan, via the Panama Canal and
Hawaii. The vessel arrived here from San Juan, Porto
Rico, where it is stated it took on about 3loo tons
of scrap iron. According to the local ship's agent,
the vessel expects to load 2680 tons of scrap iron
F/MN
at Puerto Colombia. It is stated that this commodity
comprises the entire cargo on board.
The ship's agent further advises that the vessel
expects to sail Monday afternoon, July 22nd, for
Cristobal. However, it would seem that the loading
of the scrap iron may take a day or two longer, de-
pending on the rapidity of the work.
For the Department's information there is at-

Puerto Colombia, Colombia,

SCRAP IRON SHIPMENT
At daybreak a ship entered port and at about
10:30 a.m. she came alongside the pier. I was in-
formed by the Customs Officials that this ship's name
is BOR, that she is of Yugoslav nationality, has a
crew of 32 men of which 30 are Yugoslavs, one is a
Finn and one is Norwegian. This ship, I am told,
comes from Puerto Rico, has a load on board of 3,1o0
tons of scrap iron and is oing to receive here about
3,ooo tons of scrap iron (will probably not be able
to hold more than 2,7oo tons I am informed). I met
a man who was with the ship's consignee (young
Eduardo Gerlein) who told me that he has come by air
from Canada to handle this shipment of scrap iron,
that he is a Canadian and that his name is Deutch (?).
ne is staying at the Irado rotel in arranquilla.

July 25, 1940
Honorable Lansdale Sasscer
House of Representatives
Washington, D. C.
Dear Mr. Sasscer:
We wish to register our strong protest against the
sale of 300,000 tons of scrap 1ron to Japan. Scrap 1ron
1s one of the two most vital raw materials in our busi-
ness of which there 1s already a serious shortage of the
country today.
Roughly 60% of our output goes e1ther directly or
1ndirectly to the United States Government and the balance
goes into machinery, almost all of which 1s being used in
our rearmament program. If scrap 1ron is allowed to be
exported in such enormous guantities, especially to
aggressor nations who are our potential enemies, we will
be guilty of deliberately nlacing the weepons of war into
the hands of our enemies and denriving our own armed
forces of what they so desnerately need.
We consider the additional economic burden which this
exportation will place on us as well as on all the other
steel and iron producers in the country of secondary 1m-
portance to the grave harm it will do to our own country
in the face of this national emergency and world cris1s.
Yours very truly,
LEVERING BROTHERS, Inc.
J. P. WADE LEVERING
President

894.20/986
I have the hpnor to refer to my confidential
despatch No. 193 of July 18, 1940 (File 866.12/800),
on the above subject and to inform the Department
at this time that the Yugoslav S.3. "BoR" sailed
from Puerto Colombia last night at 1l.45 p.m. bound
for Japan via Cristobal and Hawaii.
When the BOR arrived in Puerto Colombia it had
3lo0 tons of scrap iron loaded at San Juan, Puerto Rico,
and it took on board in Puerto Colombia 268o tons of
scrap iron. The crew of the vessel at the time of
arrival consisted of the Yugoslav Master Captain
R. Popovic, ten officers and twenty-two seamen, all
of whom are of Yugoslav nationality except two coal-
passers, one Finnish, the other Norwegian.
In Puerto Colombia the Captain signed on a
German, Friedrich Niemann, as a coal-passer. This
seaman is of German nationality and race, 28 years
of age and declared seven years service as a seaman.
It is said that he was born in Brazil or else taken
there as a child, and that his parents reside in
1940
Brazil. Niemann speaks Portuguese, and his Spanish
has a Portuguese accent. He is understood to have
deserted the German vessel "Sesostris" in Venezuela
last year, before the outbreak of war in Europe.
It is reported that he came overland to Colombia,
entering through Cúcuta on a tourist visa. He has
been in Puerto Colombia several months, for the most
part without employment. There has been no report
of suspicious activities on his part.
While it is

=== Page 228 === (confidence 0.99)
- 2 -
While it is understood that visitors on board
the S. S. BOR were prohibited, it has been learned
by Vice Consul Phelan that Emil Pruefert, who is
the head of the Nazi party for all Colombia, went
on board the BOR last Saturday. It has not been
possible to learn the purpose of his visit, but it
is known that he made several unsuccessful efforts
to get on board without a permit and finally was
compelled to return to Barranquilla to secure a
visitor's permit from the Customs authorities.
It seems worthy of mention that Sender Deutsch,
representative of the charterers Loudee Iron and
Metal Compeny, Inc. 30 Church St., New York City,
arrived in Barranquilla by way of Aruba to observe
the loading of the scrap iron in Puerto Colombia.
Mr. Deutsch is a naturalized Canadian, born in
Austria; he claimed to have lived in Canada since
early childhood. He stated that his company, of
which he is a partner, has offices established in
Montreal, Canada, under the name of Deitcher Brothers,
and that their principal business is dealing in
scrap iron.
The American Embassies in Panamá and Bogotá
have been telegraphically notified this morning of
the departure of the "BOR" for Cristobal.
Respectfully yours,
Nelson P. ark
Nelson R. Park

m.
BRILLION IRON WORKS INC.
Brillion, Wisconsin
July 25, 1940
Hon. Alexander Wiley,
U. S. Senate Bldg.,
Washington, D. C.
Dear Senator Wiley:
The continued exportation of scrap iron to Japan and
other foreign countries has created cons1derable hard-
ship on foundries, more especially those of a smaller
nature.
All during this period when scrap was exported very
freely, and more especially some months ago when the
foreign countries were using sizeable amount of scrap,
we were penalized by having to pay a premium of from
$1.50 to 3.00 per ton on cast scran, and then had dif-
ficulty in getting the material.
Taking this into conslderation as compared to existing
prices on gray iron castinge, it is just another case
of our costs being built up w1th no corresponding in-
crease in selling prices. In view of this shortage in
scrap, as well as the increased price we have had to
pay, we cannot understand why we should allow the sale
and exportation of scran 1ron to Japan and other foreign
countries. If this condition does not correct itself,
together with many similar problems, we can readily see
1t will not be long before many of the smaller industries
will be out of the nicture.
We do hope you will use every effort you can to prevent
the exportation of scrap, as surely this would enable us
to correct the existing conditions. At the same time, 1f
we are going to have the increased demand for cestings in
the near future on the armarent nrogrem, it will ageravate
the situation Just that much more.
Yours very truly,
BRILLION IRON WORKS Inc.
R. D. PETERS
General Manager
RDPeters/eg

My dear Secretary Welles:
During the past few days I have received a number of
letters and telegrams from foundry companies and steel-work
concerns in Kansas and the Middle West, protesting against
the continued shipment of scrap iron to Japan. The accom-
panying telegrams are typical of many that have reached me.
It is altogether likely th:t the facilities of all of
8
these concerns vil! be utilized in the development of some
394.24/100
phase of the defense program. It is their feeling, therefore,
that the utmost care should be taken to conserve the essential
raw materials of the country for defense. They feel, therefore,
that it is & mistake to continue to ship scran iron and
scrap metals out of this country and in that way lessen our
capacity for the manufacturing of ecuioment and munitions for
the defense progrem. It seems t me that their contention is
worthy of serious consideretion.
00
I am aware that the President has issued a proclamation
extending export licensing control to pftroleum products and
scrap metals. The anplication of the licensing progran to
scrap iron may tend to relleve to some extent the situation
complained of by the como:nies vhose telegrams I enclose. It
is not believed, hovever, that the situation vill be wholly
taken care of until an embargo is imposed ag inst the shipment
of scran iron and scran metals to Japan. I say this not only
because such material: are badly needed for monuf: cturing pur-
poses in the aavaneement of our own defense progrem, but for
the further reason that such an embargo would end the contri-
bution which our country nas hecn making to the Jananese
invasion of Chine through the sale of scrap metels to that
country. I am sure that an overwhelming majority of the people
of this nation would velcome an embargo against the further sate
of scrap iron and scran metals to Japan, and I should therefere
be glad if the State Lenartment could find it rossible to
F/FC
recommend an embargo ag in:t such shipments.
Cordlyy vours

=== Page 254 === (confidence 0.99)
WESTERN UNION
JUL PM 9 06
KANSAS CITY MO JULY 25 194O 547PM
HON ARTHUR CAPPER
UNITED STATES SENATOR WASHDC
WE UNDERSTAND SCRAP EXPORTERS ARE NEGOTIATING SALE OF
3OO, OOO TONS OF SCRAP TO JAPAN FOR EXPORTATION DURING THE
PRESENT QUARTER. WE RESPECTFULLY PROTEST THE SALE AND
EXPORTATION OF THIS MATERIAL THAT IS VITAL TO OUR OWN
DEFENSE PROGRAM. OUR COMPANY WITH STEEL PLANTS AT KANSAS
CITY TULSA AND STLOUIS EMPLOYING OVER 3OOO MEN ARE DEPENDENT
UPON SCRAP AS CUR ONLY SOURCE OF IRON. THE ORDNANCE DEPART-
MENT HAVE INDICATED THEY WILL EXPECT U3 TO MANUFACTURE
SHELLS AT THE RATE OF 1O, OOO TONS MONTHLY AND A SHORTAGE OF
SCRAP WILL MAKE IT IMPOSSIBLE FOR US TO FULFILL THIS OBLIGA-
TION. A NUMBER OF OTHER INDEPENDENT STEEL PRODUCERS IN
THE MIDDLE WESTERN AREA ALSO ARE DEPENDENT UPON SCRAP AS
THEIR PRINCIPAL RAW MATERIAL. A SHORTAGE OF SCRAP WILL
ALSO AFFECT THEIR USEFULNESS IN ANY EMERGENCY. JAPAN
ALONE HAS TAKEN FROM THIS COUNTRY DURING THE PAST TEN
YEARS OVER SIX MILLION TONS OF SCRAP OR APPROXIMATELY
75 PERCENT OF HER TOTAL SCRAP IMPORTS. CONSIDERING HOW
LIBERALLY WE HAVE CONTRIBUTED TO JAPANS PRESENT BACKLOG
SHE CAN HARDLY NOW OBJECT TO AN EMBARGO ON THT3 RAW
MATERIAL SO VITALLY IMPORTANT TO US. EVEN THOUGH WE
CONSIDER NO EMERGENCY EXISTS AN EMBARGO ON SCRAP WILL
CAUSE FOREIGN COUNTRIES NEEDING STEEL TO BUY FINISHED
STEEL PRODUCTS INSTEAD OF SCRAP AND AS IT TAKES APPROXI-
MATELY TWENTY MAN HOURS PER TON TO CONVERT SCRAP INTO
FINISHED STEEL IT IS EASY TO ESTIMATE TO WHAT EXTENT
DOMESTIC LABOR WILL BE HELPED BY SUCH AN EMBARGO AND
FINALLY WE ARE THE ONLY COUNTRY IN THE WORLD PERMITTING
THE EXPORTATION OF SCRAP.
SHEFFIELD STEEL CORP.

=== Page 261 === (confidence 0.99)
15West Beech Street,
Long Beach, New York,
July 20, 1940
Secretary of the Treasury
Washington, D. C.
Dear Sir:
As a Political science student at the University
of Nisconsin, I am naturally interested in some of
the things the Covernment is doing. One cannot anal-
yse until one knows the arguments for and against
actions. Therefore, J am writing to ask you some
questions upon matters which I find hard to justify.
First:
: Why has the United States been storing
most of the world's gold at Fort Knox when there is
a prave danger that the world may go off the gold
standard and leave us holding the bag, so to speak?
Is the U. S. still doing this?
Second: Why have we continued to sell war mater-
ials to Japan with merely a "moral embargo" when she
is conducting as cruelly an aggressive war as is
Hitler? Should not our neutrality act apply to her?
And is it true that we are sending her large amounts
of scran iron? Is so, why?
I trust that you will see the reasons for my
asking these questions. I do not wish to condemn, and
most of all condemn without reason. I would apprec-
iate a reply in the near future.
Yours truly
IRENE EDNARDS

STRICTLY CONFIDENTIAL
Goorge A. Makinson, Esquire,

American Embassy,
Tokyo.
Sir:
With reference to the Consulate General's telegram
of May 22, 5 p.m., and this Consulate's reply of May 23,
4 p.m., there is submitted herewith an allegedly complete
list of all the tin smelters and refineries in Japan
which has just been made available to this Consulate by
a reliable foreign source.
The Consulate's source has stated that only the
first mentioued firm is of any importance and that it
sccounts for the bulk of Japanese production. In l936,
it is said that 94% of all the tin amelted in Japan came
from this concern. Practically all the other firms men-
tioned do not produce tin from raw ore but recover it from
tin sheet scrap, old sardine cans, etc., according to this
source. It has, to date, been impossible to procure any
rocent figures concerning the amount or value of the pro-
duction of melters in this district.
Very truly yours,
John M. Allison,
American Consul.

STRICTLY CONFIDENTIAL
George A. Makinson, Esquire,
First Secretary of American Embassy,
Tokyo, Japan.
Sir:
Referenoe is made to your telegram of May 22,
5 p.m., l940, inquiring in regard to the existence
of smelters of wolfram or tin in the Kwantung Leased
Territory or Manohuria and to the possibility that
unrefined ores from Bolivia or other South Amerioan
countries are being transshipped for despateh to
Germany, and to this office's letter of June 12, 1940,
enclosing a report in response to such inquiry.
As of interest in conneotion with the above-
mentioned matter, I have to report that I have just
learned from a thoroughly reliable source that ship-
ments of Chinese tungsten ore to Germany via Man-
churia and the trans-Siberian Railway have been going
through during recent months. Information concerning
the volume of such shipments could not be obtained.
I gathered from the informant's remarks (but did not
feel it tactful to attempt to verify by close ques-
tioning) that the shipments have for some reason come
through north China rather than being despatched direct
to Dairen from south China.
Respectfully yours,
A. S. Chase
American Consul

PARTICIPANTS: Mr. Morito Morishima, Counselor, Japanese Embassy;

Tohyo

MR. HORBtCK
JUL181940
844.24
DE PARTMENT OF STATE
11400
Mr. Morishima, Counselor of the Japanese Embassy,
called at his request, and brought with him Mr. Saneyoshi,
President, Japan Gasoline Company, who ha recently arrived
in this country from Japan for the purpose of representing
his company in the law suit which the Japan Gasoline
Company 1s bringing against the Universal O1l Products
C_
C.
FIL
Compeny.
io
9
in the case involving the two contracts which the Uiversal
Oil Products Company has with the Japan Gasoline Company,
and added little information to what is already known to
the Department.
F/N
With respect to the l928 contract by which Universal
turns over to the Japan Gasoline Company certain technical
information

=== Page 321 === (confidence 0.99)
-2-
information for the construction of cracking plants for the
production of motor gasoline, Mr. Saneyoshi exhibited an
exchange of cablegrams between himself and Mr. Halle,
President, Universal Oil Products Company. A cablegram
from Mr. Halle, dated April 30, 1940, stated that within
three weeks plans for cracking processes would be turned
over to Mitsui and Company, New York, for the Japan
Gasoline Company. A further cablegram from Mr. Halle,
dated May 24, stated that the situation had changed so
substantially that it would be impossible to supply the
necessary information. Mr. Morishima said that there must
be ome misunderstanding upon Mr. Halle's part as it had
been previously recognized by all concerned that the crack-
ing process in question was not included in the scope of
the moral embargo. Mr. Saneyoshi pointed out that technical
knowledge of cracking processes was generally known in the
trade, and that a process could be copied from equipment
owned by the Mitsubishi interests, although he preferred to
obtain the data under the terms of the Universal contract.
With regard to the 1938 contract for the release of
informetion for the manufacture of high octane gasoline,
Mr. Saneyosh1 explained that the Japan Gasoline Company
was being subjected to serious financial losses as a result
of Universal's failure to furnish further technical data.
He

=== Page 322 === (confidence 0.95)
-3
He stated that his company had, in turn, many contracts
with Japanese firms to supply them with informaticn or
produots which would be forthooming under the Japan
Gasoline Company's contract with Universal. He said that
if Universal could supply the information for the crack-
1ng processes under the 1928 contract and such data under
the 1938 contract as would not involve the release of
knowledge for the production of aviation gasoline itself,
he might be able to mollify his clients to some extent.
Mr. Hamilton said that the Department was glad to
hear Mr. Saneyoshi's personal presentation of the case
and that careful note would be made of the facts as
presented. Mr. Hamilton added that the Department was not
in a position at present to add anything new to what
Mr. Berle had told the Japanese Ambassador at the latter's
visit on July 9.

The Japanese Ambassador called to see me today,

them to complete this contract.
The Ambassador expressed his gratitude and said
that he would discuss the matter with the Japan
Gasolene Company in this sense.
Note:- Prior to this interview, and after discussing
the matter with Mr. Hamilton and Mr. Hackworth, I telephoned
Colonel Maxwell about this contract. Colonel Maxwell
stated: (1) that he and the Defense Commission had had
this matter up and had decided, on principle, that they
saw no reason arising out of the national defense to
place an embargo on the export of the materials necessary
to fulfill this contract; (2) that he, as Administrator
could see no end to be gained by impeding fulfillment of
this contract, and () that it was their belief that probably
the processes could be quite adequately obtained elsewhere.
In any event, so far as defense purposes were concerned,
they were not prepared to legalize any "moral embargo"
in this case. He offered to take it up with the Defense
Commission again, if I requested it, but added that
he

Dear Sir:
We at this time are particularly concerned and are
protesting sale and exportation to Japan of approximately
300,000 tons of sorap steel and iron.
There is no doubt as to a shortage of this material at
the present time, and, in the face of a National
emergency, will impose additional economic burden on
all iron and steel producers.
We are asking you at this time to lend us whatever
assistance you can in order to stop this additional
tonnage from being exported, thereby preserving our
resources for the United States of Amerioa, first, last
and always.
Very truly yours,
Enterprise Foundry Co., Ino.

Reference a memorandum prepared by the
Chinese Counoil for Economic Research, June

The memorandum under reference is, in my
opinion, well done. Beginning on page 13 and

U. S. Exports to Japan of Scrap Iron and Steel
American merohants sold to Japan in 1939 over 2 million tons (2,026,764) of
scrap iron and steel valued at almost 33 million dollars ($32,592,500)1
The
sorap exports to Japan were larger in 1939 than in any previous year, due undoubt-
edly to rush orders by Japan in anticipation of a possible ombargo following the
894.
termination of the Japanese-Amorican trade treaty in January, 1940.
In 1937, the first year of the Sino-Japanese War, the shipments of iron and
steel scrap to Japan greatly advanced over the average for the three previous
years. The following index numbers (Average 1934-36 = 100) graphically reveal
the trend of U. S. shipments for the three years prior to the War and for the
subsequent yoars.

The trend of U. S. shipments of scrap iron and steel to Japan in 1940 has
been downward. For the first 3 months of 1940 shipments were less by 50 percent
than they were for the same period in l939. The scrap exports to Japan from Jan-
uary through March amounted to 526,000 tons in 1939 and to 252,000 tons in 1940.
The urgent need of Japan to conserve its foreign exchange probably accounts for
the reduced purchases of scrap by Japan during the early months of 1940.
During the 15 yours prior to 1.2), Canada was the largest market for Ameri-
can exports of scrap iron and steel. 2

Japan.
The proportion of scrap exports to the total U. S. exports to Japan has been
inoreasing in recent years. The value of the American scrap exports to Japan ad-
vanced from 6.3 percent of the total U. S. exports to Japan during the years
1934-1936 to 13.7 percent in 1937, 9.2 percent in 1938 and 14.1 percent in 1939.
Sorap is important in the manufacture of iron and steel in the United States.
The quantities of scrap in relation to the ore used in the iron and steel industry
in this country demonstrates the importance of this product in the American economy.
Aocording to a report issued by the Bureau of Mines,
"The total scrap consumed in 1938 was equivalent to 132 percent
of the iron content of all domestic and foreign iron and manganifer-
ous iron ores used in blast furnaces, and purchased scrap alone equaled
62 percent of the iron content of the ores; in l937 the comparable per-
centages were 119 and 57."
The report further points out the increased use of scrap in American industry
when it states that:
"In making the average ton of steel in 1938 more scrap and less
pig iron were used than in 1937; a decline in the relative use of pur-
chased scrap was more than offset by an increase in the relative use
of home scrap. In iron furnaces the relative use of purchased scrap
increased, that of home scrap changed little, and that of pig iron de-
creased."1
The Bureau of Mines made a survey in the fall of 1939 of the supply within
the United States of iron and steel scrap and reported 7,220,O00 gross tons on
hand as of September 30, 1939. The Bureau further reported scrap consumption
for the month amounting to 3,078,100 gross tons "indicating known stocks held by
suppliers and consumers were equivalent to a ten week's supply."
It appears that under ordinary peace time circumstances, the United States
is not confronted with any major problem of adequate supply. However, large
scale exports of iron and steel scrap would be seriously reflected in "a national
1/
Statistical and Economic Survey, M M S No. 770. dated Dec. 4. 1939, Bureau of
Mines.

orgonoy requiring peak production". Iron foundries conveivably would be "handi-

critical periods, the country was confronted with a shortage of scrap.1
Japan's Import of Scrap Iron and Steel
Japan depended upon non-yen foreign countries for over 50% of its total
scrap consumption. Two and a quarter million tons of iron and steel scrap valued
at 36 million dollars were imported into Japan in 1939from 14 countries.2
Almost nine-tenths (89 percent) of the tonnage (as distinct from value) came
from the United States. A dozen other countries furnished tho remaining 10
percent.
Since the beginning of the Far Eastern War, the United States' share of the
quantity of scrap iron and steel exports to Japan increased from 84 percent in
1937, to 86 percent in 1938 and to 89 percent in 1939. The following table pre-

ap in the Japanese Iron and Steel Industry
Sorap is derived from outworn iron and steel products and from wastes
which accumlate in the course of production. Heavy consumption of scrap is
characteristic of iron and steel manufacture in Japan. This is explained by
the preponderance of open-hearth furnaces for which scrap is used in high
percentages because of economic reasons. The use of scrap in Japan began to
increase in 1932 until an impressive total of 3,337,000 metric tons was re-
corded for the year 1936. This trend is considerably accelorated under the
stimulus of the prosent expansionist policy followed by the heavy industrics.1
Concerning the importan@ of scrap for the Japaneso steel industry, the United
States Bureau of Minos in thc Mincral Yearbook for 1939 states that,
"The production of steel ingots and casting in Japan
has more than doubled since 1932, and the domand for ferrous
raw materials has incroased corrospondingly. Pig-iron pro-
duction has boen oxpandod to mect part of the requiroments;
however, the proportions of scrap used in furnacc chargos
also have incrcased, and imports of scrap iron and scrap
stool aro ssential to the maintenance of the presest stool-
making rate."
The amount of scrap Japan uses in relation to pig iron in the course of
producing iron and steel products was indicated in the U. S. Commerce Dopart-
ment's Japan Monthly Trade Report of November 25, 1939, where it was stated
that in the face of an "anticipated reduction in scrap iron inports following
the expiration of the commercial treaty with the Unitod States in January, 1940,
the Japan Steel Materials Federation is reported to have drawn up a plan for the
produotion of steel without the need of large quantities of imported scrap. The
scheme cells for the use of "larger quantities of pig iron." However, "in the
meantime," tho Report continued, "the Fedcration established a maximum allot-
ment of 60% sorap and 40% pig for the fourth quarter ( of 1939 ) in order to
conserve existing stocks of raw materials. Trade reports state that the allot-
ments will be further reduced to a maximum of 50% scrap iron from the boginning
1 See Japan-Manchoukuo Year Book, 1940, p. 340.

=== Page 355 === (confidence 0.99)
6
cessation of imports from the United States, it is claimed that it would be
necessary for domestic mills to operate on a basis of 20% scrap and 80% pig."
It is very likely that Japanose steel mills are at prosont operating on tho
basis of 50% scrap and 50% pig. Any furthor drastic scaling down in the scrap
peroentago will ontail great difficultics in connection with readjustments, in
viow of tho fact that tho majority of steel manufacturing companies rely on
open-hearth furnaces, which must use scrap.1
Alternatives to American Scrap
If American iron and steel scrap should become unavailable to Japan she
would be confronted with three alternatives: (A) Replace American scrap with
other foreign scrap; (B) Curtail production; (C) Obtain pig iron and iron ore.
These alternatives are considered in the following discussion.
Alternative (A): Replacement of American Scrap with other Foreign
Scrap - Foreign scrap constitutes more than half of the scrap consumed by Japan.
Ninety percent of the crap imported into Japan comes from the United States.
The stocks of the countries furnishin Japan with scrap, with the exception of
the United States, are very limited. Moreover, since the European War, restric-
tions and prohibitions have been placeu upon the exports of scrap by most of the
countries making shipments to Japan. It is an unavoidablc conclusion that Japan
today depends more than ever upon the United States for iron and stoel scrap and
that it would be impossible for her to replace the 2,oo0,ooo tons reccived from
the United States in other markets.
Alternative (B): Curtailment of Production - E. . Pehrson of the
United States Bureau of Mines estimates that one-third of Japan's steol pro-
duction in 1937 dopended upon American scrap.2 It may be safely assumed that

=== Page 356 === (confidence 0.98)
7
this was also true of 1938 and 1939. The loss of steel output in consequence
of an American embargo on exports of scrap to Japan would play havoc with the
latter's war program. Japan's decision in the event of a cessation of scrap
supplies from the United States would probably be to make a despearte effort to
locate alternetive supplies of pig iron and iron ore in order to avoid stoppages
of production in tho war and allied industries which would greatly affect her
campaigns in China as well as her other expansionist designs.
Alternativo (C): Obtaining Pig Iron and Iron Ore to Replace American scrap

attaining a lovol of 3.3 million tons in l939.2

China - The output of pig iron in China Proper is known to be small and
no exportable surplus can be expected.

of shipments to Japan. In January 1940 Indian shipments delivered to Japan

the near future the pre-war volumes of oro supply must remain doubtful.
Suamary
The salient facts brought forth by the for going discussion may be
summarized as follows:
1. American scrap is of vital importance to the Japanese heavy industry
and to the war economy as a whole. The scrap material furnished Japan by the
United States is responsible for approximately ono-third of Japan's steel
production.
2. If Japan should be denied access to tho American scrap market, which
provides 90 percent of her imported iron and steel scrap, the required material

=== Page 363 === (confidence 0.99)
14
nd not possibly be replaced in other markets. Nor could Japan aoquire foreign
pig iron to replace the interrupted U. S. sorap supply.
3. The only alternative would be for Japan to secure the necessary iron
ore for processing pig iron herself. In so far as the foreign sources are
concerned, no prospects are in sight, at least in the near future, of any
considerably increased supply available to Japan. Under the circumstances
Japan would be compelled to exploit, at great cost, her own low-grade deposits.
To cover the loss of some 2 million tons of American scrap would require 5 to
6 million tons of domestically mined ore. This means that Japan would have to
inorease at least four-fold her current iron ore production, estimated at around
1.250,000 tons. Actually, however, it is even doubtful whether Japan could
in the near future achieve a gain of as much as double the existing output.
4. The United States under ordinary peace time circumstances is not faced
with any major problem of adequate scrap supply. However, when peak production
is required in a national emergency, as is the case with the present execution
of the national defense program, the country will be confronted with a shortage
of sorap.1
1
In fact, there is at prosent a definite need for this country to conserve
its scrap stock, as supplies are found to be already relatively scarce. (See
dispatch from Pittsburg, captioned "Long-Term Views Strong For Steel", the
New York Times, June 24, 1940, p. 25.
